How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

We conducted a direct replication of Drew et al. (2017). The experiment examines visual search behavior, including eye movements, when participants are looking for one of many memorized objects. The original experiment found that the search strategy for this kind of search, called hybrid search, involves "one visual search [and] many memory searches. " Our replication (N=38) finds strong agreement with the original (N=14) results. As the number of memorized objects increases, participants fixate longer on each individual object. Furthermore, refixations are generally rare, with fewer than half of the visual items refixated during search. We conducted this replication as part of the course Research Methods in Cognitive Science at Vassar College, and we briefly discuss the pedagogical framing of this work.

Introduction

Many everyday tasks require looking for any of several items held in memory. A shopper walking the aisles of a grocery store with a dozen items on a mental list, for instance, must scan visual displays of products while checking candidates against the list. This kind of simultaneous search of the visual environment and memory is called hybrid search (Wolfe et al., 2015).

How hybrid search is actually performed is not obvious. A variety of strategies are possible (for a review of candidate architectures for multipletarget search, see Ort & Olivers, 2020). At one extreme, a searcher might cycle through memorized targets one at a time, scanning the visual scene fully for each before moving on. At the other extreme, they might fixate each visual item once and check it against every memorized target before shifting gaze. Intermediate strategies could involve searching the display for a subset of the memorized items, conducting subsequent searches for other memorized items. These strategies make different predictions about how eye movements should change with the size of the memory set: if searchers look for subsets of memorized items, then they should require multiple visual passes through the display, and the number of passes should scale with the size of the memorized set (Drew et al., 2017). Drew et al. (2017) addressed these questions directly. They tasked participants with memo rizing a set of objects and then searching for any item from the set in a display that contained many distractors. They varied the number of items that participants had to memorize (1, 4, 16, or 100) and used eye tracking to examine how gaze behavior changes as the number of memorized items increases. Two of their results are particularly important for understanding how we perform these hybrid searches. First, replicating prior work (Wolfe, 2012), they found that search times increased logarithmically with the number of items in memory. Second, they found that participants tended to look at each visual item only one or two times, but that dwell times on those items increased with the number of memorized items. This suggests, as they highlight in their paper title, that hybrid search involves "one visual search [and] many memory searches. " This paper presents a replication attempt of this work. We attempted this replication as part of the course Research Methods in Cognitive Science at Vassar College. In the course, students and professors collaborated to plan, conduct, analyze, and publish the project, an approach with which we've had previous success (de Leeuw, Andrews, Altman, et al., 2019; de Leeuw et al., 2021; James et al., 2025). The context of conducting this replication as part of a course informed some of our choices, including why we selected Drew et al. (2017) for replication in the first place. We looked for previously unreplicated papers with low sample sizes so that our replication attempt could collect a large enough sample to be informative while operating in the constraints of a halfsemester project. Second, we wanted to select a topic (search behavior) with rich theoretical and empirical work (BellaFernández et al., 2022; Todd & Hills, 2020; Wolfe, 2013) that could form the basis of studentled projects after conducting the replication. Third, we needed a design that students could implement and run in a limited time frame. Finally, we sought to introduce the students to a methodological approach (eye tracking) beyond basic behavioral experiments. The target paper met all these criteria.

Methods

Our materials, data, analysis files, and instructions for reproducing our complete manuscript are available on Zenodo at https://zenodo.org/records/23503684. We preregistered the replication at tempt at https://osf.io/b58mc/.1 A demo of the experiment, without eye tracking or data collection, is available to try at https://jodeleeuw.github.io/219-2026-replication/experiment/. The study was approved (as exempt) by the Vassar College Institutional Review Board. Participants 38 Vassar College students (average age 19.9 years; 12 male, 24 female, 2 nonbinary) completed the experiment. This matched our preregistered analytic sample size. We set a target sample size of 38 based, in part, on the heuristic of 2.5x the original sample size (n=14) suggested by Simonsohn (2015). However, with 19 students in the class, we increased the sample from 35 to 38 so that each student would have the opportunity to run data collection twice.

Prior to testing, participants completed two vision screenings: the Ishihara Color Test to assess color vision deficiencies and the Safe Eyes America Home Vision Test to confirm visual acuity. Only participants who passed both screenings proceeded to the experiment. Therefore, all participants had normal or correctedtonormal vision (at least 20/25 visual acuity) and were screened for color vision deficiencies, with no significant color blindness detected. Upon completing the experiment, each participant received $20.

Procedure

The experiment included four blocks, each using a different memory set size (MSS): 1, 4, 16, or 100 unique objects (see Materials below for details on the objects). We randomized block order across participants. Each block proceeded through three phases in a fixed order -memorization, recognition, and search. Figure 1 shows the sequence of one trial in each of these phases. During recognition, participants saw every memorized target plus an equal number of foils one at a time, responding by keypress; they had to pass two such tests at 90% accuracy or better before moving on. During search, they clicked the memorized object when one was present or the "Absent" button at the left of the screen when none was. The search task shows scenes from the experiment at actual scale relative to the screen; the text and images of the other panels are not to scale in order to facilitate readability.

Each block began with a memorization phase. After a brief 2 second "study the following images carefully" message, each object appeared one at a time for 3 seconds in the center of the screen. Participants attempted to memorize all of the images.

After memorization, participants completed a recognition test to verify that they had learned the targets. Each test contained every memorized target plus an equal number of foils, presented one at a time in random order. On each trial, a single object appeared and participants pressed P if it was one of the memorized targets or A if it was not. To advance to the search task, participants had to pass two recognition tests at ≥90% accuracy within the block. A failed test was followed by another presentation of the memory set and another test, while a passed test that was not yet the second pass was followed directly by another test without representing the memory set. The two passes did not need to be consecutive: a failure between two passes did not reset the count of prior passes. Foils used in earlier tests within a block were excluded from later tests in that block until the foil pool was exhausted. To save time, we stopped any individual recognition test immediately once it became mathematically impossible to reach 90% accuracy on that test. Experimenters used their judgment on when to abort the experiment if participants failed a memory test after multiple attempts. This was not a preregistered exclusion criteria, but became necessary to avoid over taxing our participants. Three participants failed to pass the N=100 memory test after multiple attempts, and did not complete the experiment. Their data are not included in the analysis, and are not counted in the sample of 38. Among the 38 participants included in the analysis, 18 took the minimum of two attempts, 17 took three attempts, and 3 took four attempts.

Once participants met the memorization criterion, they began the hybrid search task. On each search trial, either one memorized item was present or none were. Participants clicked on the memorized item to complete the trial. If there was no target present in the display, participants clicked a button indicating that none of the objects were a target (Figure 1). The display contained either 8 or 16 objects (the visual set size, or VSS) varying randomly trialbytrial within each block, with half the trials at each VSS. A target object appeared on 50% of trials. Items were placed at randomly selected cells of a 5 × 5 grid spanning a search region that occupied 90% of the screen width and 80% of the screen height; each item was rendered at 10% of the viewport's smaller dimension (approximately 108 pixels, or ≈2.8° of visual angle on our display). Within each block, distractor images were sampled without replacement, so no distractor appeared on more than one trial. A 1 second central fixation cross separated search trials. Each block contained 80 trials.

Before the first main block, participants completed a practice session to learn the task. The practice session began with a 6item memorization phase and accompanying recognition test, using the same ≥90%accuracy / twopass criterion as the main blocks, followed by 12 practice search trials that crossed VSS (8 vs. 16) with target presence (3 trials per cell). Unlike main block trials, practice search trials provided immediate accuracy feedback (a green "Correct!" or red "Wrong!" message for 1 second) after each response. The 6 practice memorization targets and the practice distractors were drawn from a separate image pool; no image used in practice was also used in a main block (see Materials).

Apparatus and Calibration

We used a Tobii Pro X3120 eye tracker, which measures eye movements at 120 Hz. The eye tracker connected to a Lenovo ThinkStationE31 25532C2 desktop equipped with an Intel Xeon E31275 V2 processor, 20 GB of DDR3 memory, and Windows 10 Enterprise. The monitor used was a ViewSonic VG2448A. The monitor has a 24 inch IPS screen, 60 Hz refresh rate, and 1920 × 1080 pixel resolution.

For calibration, participants sat in an adjustable chair directly in front of the display. We adjusted chair and monitor height to align each participant's eyes with the center of the screen and establish a consistent viewing distance. An onscreen procedure verified both eyetracker accuracy and viewing distance: a face stimulus appeared, with the eyes turning green when the participant was 57-63 cm from the center of the screen and red otherwise.

The full calibration and validation routine consisted of the viewingdistance check described above, a 9point calibration, and a 9point validation requiring mean gaze error ≤ 0.05 in normalized screen coordinates (≈2.5° horizontally, ≈1.4° vertically at our viewing distance), averaged across the nine points, with the routine repeated if validation did not succeed. We ran this routine immediately before each set of search trials: once after the practice memorization and recognition phases and before the practice search trials, and once within each of the four main blocks, after that block's memorization and recognition phases. Participants therefore completed at least five calibration/validation cycles over the course of the experiment.

Materials

We wrote the experiment code in jsPsych (de Leeuw, 2015; de Leeuw et al., 2023), using a custom adapter to connect jsPsych with the Tobii system via the Tobii SDK (de Leeuw, 2026).

Stimuli for the main blocks came from the 2,400 unique object images used by Brady et al. (2008), available at https://bradylab.ucsd.edu/stimuli.html. We used the entire set: 560 of the 2,400 images were set aside as the pool from which memorization targets and recognitiontest foils were drawn, and the remaining 1,840 served as search distractors. The distractor images were divided at random into four groups of 460, and each of the four memory set size blocks was assigned two of these groups (920 distractors), such that each pair of blocks shared either 460 distractors or none. The 920 distractors assigned to a block are exactly the number needed to fill that block's 80 search trials without repeating a distractor. The assignment of images to the memorization pool and to the four distractor groups was made once, with a script, and then held fixed for every participant; block order was randomized per participant, as described above.

Practice images came from a separate set: the 400 objects (200 large, 200 small) collected by Konkle & Oliva (2012) and distributed by the Konk Lab at Harvard (https://konklab.fas.harvard.edu). The practice memorization targets and the practice search distractors were both sampled from this set, so no image file used in practice was also used in a main block. However, we discovered after completing the experiment that the two sets are not fully independent. 131 of the 400 practice images are differently cropped versions of a photograph that also appears in the Brady et al. (2008) set (106 of them of a distractor, 25 of an image in the memorization pool). Participants could therefore encounter the same object in practice and in a main block, although they never saw the identical image twice.

Although Drew et al. did not explicitly specify the source of their stimulus images, we selected the images from Brady et al. (2008) because it contains many of the same object images that appear in the Drew et al. (2017) publication, and is potentially the exact image set used in the original experiment.

Deviations from the original study

We deviated from the original study in a few ways: 1. We reduced the number of visual search trials per block from 160 to 80. We did this mainly due to time constraints. Without this modification, the experiment duration would have been too long for our scheduling process. We also worried about participant fatigue. The experiment took about an hour as we ran it; any longer felt like a difficult ask for people to maintain concentration. 2. We included one 12trial practice, rather than 12 practice trials per block. This was also done to reduce the overall length. 3. Our equipment differed from the original. We used a Tobii X3120 recording binocularly at 120Hz; the original used an EyeLink 1000 recording one eye at 500Hz. Our participants did not use a chin rest, while the original method did. 4. The original used an Eyelink algorithm for fixation detection. We used a datadriven method from Engbert & Kliegl (2003) combined with filtering for a minimum duration and movement between sequential fixations. 5. Our screen size was slightly larger than the original, so we modified the size of the Areas of Interest (AOIs) to be approximately the same in terms of visual angle. 6. There was one place of ambiguity in the methods of the original. The methods section of Drew et al. (2017) reports that participants "were each tested on five blocks, each with a different MSS of 1, 4, 16, 32, or 100 unique objects. " However, none of the visualizations contain data for a memory set size of 32, and the number of trials reported doesn't make sense unless there were only four blocks. Therefore, we ultimately concluded that testing memory set sizes of 1, 4, 16, and 100 was the most faithful replication.

Results

We conducted all analyses in R (R Core Team, 2024) using the afex package (Singmann et al., 2025) for repeatedmeasures ANOVAs; ggplot2 (Wickham et al., 2025), patchwork (Pedersen, 2025), and ggpath (Carl, 2025) for figures; the dplyr (Wickham et al., 2026), tidyr (Wickham et al., 2024), stringr (Wickham, 2023), purrr (Wickham & Henry, 2023), and jsonlite (Ooms, 2023) packages for data manipulation; here (Müller, 2025) for path management; osfr (Wolen & Hartgerink, 2022) for retrieving data from OSF; and knitr (Xie, 2025) within Quarto to compile the manuscript. Throughout the results, we report generalized eta squared (𝜂 2 𝐺 ) as a measure of standardized effect size. To facilitate direct comparison of our replication with the original, we also report the 𝜂 2 𝐺 for the original as 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 when it was reported in the original. Not all results in the original included a report of effect size.

Search time and accuracy

Accuracy

Before examining patterns in response times and eye movements, we first verified that participants performed the search task accurately. Overall, participants were 95.76% accurate (SD = 2.00, min = 90.62). No participants were excluded for accuracy rates at or below 50%.

To assess how accuracy varied with memory load and target presence, we conducted a 4 (MSS: 1, 4, 16, 100) × 2 (target present vs. absent) repeatedmeasures ANOVA on each participant's mean error rate. As shown in Figure 2 B, participants were more accurate on target absent than target present trials, F(1, 37) = 83.23, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .28, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .26,2 and more accurate for smaller memorized set sizes, F(2.33, 86.06) = 41.08, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .26, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .52. There was also a significant interaction between MSS and target presence, F(2.2, 81.45) = 12.69, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .11, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .05: errors increased more steeply with MSS on target present trials than on target absent trials. Despite these effects, accuracy remained high across all conditions, replicating the high accuracy reported in the original study and giving us confidence that the response time and eyetracking data reflect successful search.

Response time

A central question of the original study was how response time scales with memory load. Linear growth in response time would suggest that participants compare each visual item against memory in a serial, exhaustive manner, while logarithmic growth would imply a more efficient memory search process (Wolfe, 2012). To address this question, we examined response times on correct trials as a function of MSS and target presence.

We computed each participant's mean response time on correct trials in each combination of MSS (1, 4, 16, 100) and target presence, then analyzed these means with a 4 × 2 repeatedmeasures ANOVA. As shown in Figure 2 A, participants were faster on target present than target absent trials, F(1, 37) = 187.44, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .38, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .25, and faster for smaller memorized set sizes, F(2.33, 86.37) = 203.41, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .58, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .52. The MSS × target presence interaction was also significant, F(2.57, 94.97) = 49.49, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .09, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .04. Crucially, the rise in response times across MSS clearly flattened as the memory set grew, consistent with a logarithmic rather than linear relationship between memory load and search time. To make this comparison concrete and following Drew et al. (2017), we fit the groupmean response times at MSS of 1, 4, and 16, separately for each combination of target presence and visual set size, with both a linear and a logarithmic function of MSS, and used each model to extrapolate to MSS = 100 (open shapes in Figure 2 A). The logarithmic predictions track the observed values much more closely than the linear ones, replicating the key behavioral result of Drew et al. (2017).

Search slope

The search slope -how much each additional item in the visual display adds to the response time -provides a measure of search efficiency. If memorizing more items causes participants to spend longer at each fixated item, then search slopes should grow with MSS.

For each participant and each combination of MSS and target presence, we estimated the search slope as the coefficient of visual set size in the withinsubject regression of response time on visual set size. We then analyzed these slopes with a 4 × 2 repeatedmeasures ANOVA. As shown in Figure 2 C, slopes were steeper on target absent than target present trials, F(1, 37) = 131.71, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .38, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .28, consistent with the standard observation that target absent trials require an exhaustive search of the display. Slopes also grew with MSS, F(2.27, 83.83) = 102.43, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .42, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .47, with a larger increase on target absent trials, as reflected in a significant MSS × target presence interaction, F(2.58, 95.46) = 13.01, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .05. The growth in search slopes with MSS is consistent with participants spending more time at each visual location as memory load increases -a key prediction of the "one visual search, many memory searches" account that we examine more directly with the eyetracking data below.

2Reported degrees of freedom for withinsubjects effects with more than one numerator degree of freedom are GreenhouseGeisser corrected.

Eye tracking data

The behavioral results show that response times and search slopes both grow with memory load, but they do not, on their own, distinguish between two qualitatively different accounts of how the search is performed: a single visual scan of the display in which each fixation involves checking the item against the entire memory set, or repeated visual scans of the display, one per memorized target. The eyetracking data let us discriminate between these accounts directly. The singlesearch account predicts that the rate of distractor revisits should be roughly stable across MSS, while dwell time on each fixated item should increase with increases in MSS. The repeatedsearch account predicts the opposite: stable dwell times alongside large increases in revisits.

Fixation patterns

Fixation processing. The Tobii X3120 recorded samples at 120 Hz in screennormalized coordi nates, which we converted to pixel coordinates using the 1920 × 1080 resolution of the display. We then classified samples into fixations and saccades on each trial using the velocitybased algorithm of Engbert & Kliegl (2003). For each sample, we computed the x and y components of gaze velocity over a fivesample window and estimated the standard deviation of each velocity component using a medianbased estimator that is robust to outlying samples. We classified a sample as a saccade if its velocity vector fell outside an elliptical threshold in twodimensional velocity space, with each axis scaled by a factor of 6 times its standard deviation; otherwise we classified it as belonging to a fixation. We then grouped runs of consecutively classified samples into events and discarded the saccade events, retaining each fixation event's median (x, y) position and its duration. Following the defaults of Tobii's IVT fixation filter (Olsen, 2012), we then merged adjacent fixations separated by less than 75 ms and less than 0.5° of visual angle, and discarded fixations shorter than 60 ms. This final step was not preregistered, and running the analysis without it produces the same pattern of results. However, without this step there are several thousand fixations that are less than 16ms, which are clearly misidentified by the Engbert & Kliegl (2003) algorithm we preregistered.

To link fixations to items in the search display, we used a 140 × 140 pixel area of interest centered on each item, slightly larger than each item's rendered footprint of approximately 108 × 108 pixels. We treated any such fixation that fell within this AOI as a fixation on the corresponding item in the analyses below. Following Drew et al. (2017), we restricted all eyetracking analyses to trials with a correct response, excluding 515 of 12160 search trials (4.2%).

Dwell time.

A core prediction of the "one visual search, many memory searches" account is that as the number of memorized items grows, the time spent evaluating each fixated item should grow with it: every item must be checked against more entries in memory before search moves on. A repeatedsearch account makes a different prediction. If participants scan the display more than once, comparing each item against only part of the memory set on any one pass, then the time spent on an item during a single look need not change with MSS. Drew et al. (2017) describe their eyetracking measures as including cumulative dwell time: the durations of all fixations an item received on a trial, summed so that an item fixated three times contributes one total rather than three observations. They report it averaged both over every distractor in the display, so that distractors that were never fixated contribute zero, and over fixated distractors only. A cumulative measure of this kind cannot distinguish the two accounts, because both predict that it will grow with MSS. Under a single visual search with longer memory comparisons, the total grows because each look at an item lasts longer. Under repeated visual searches, it grows because each additional pass through the display contributes another fixation to the same item, so time accumulates on an item as MSS increases even if no individual look ever lengthens. Averaging over every distractor adds a third source of growth: that version also rises whenever more distractors are fixated at all. We therefore measured dwell time per fixation. For each distractor that a participant fixated on a trial, we averaged the durations of the fixations it received; for each participant we then averaged these peritem values within each MSS × target presence cell and analyzed the resulting means with a 4 × 2 repeatedmeasures ANOVA. This approach deviates from our preregistered plan to measure cumulative dwell time. However, based on the logic above, we think this is a critical change which we did not realize until after data analysis was underway.

As shown in Figure 3 B, dwell time per fixation grew with MSS, F(1.75, 64.93) = 72.71, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .37, rising from approximately 178 ms at MSS = 1 to approximately 252 ms at MSS = 100 on target absent trials. Fixations were also longer on target absent than target present trials, F(1, 37) = 72.98, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .06, and the MSS × target presence interaction was small, F(1.54, 57) = 4.16, p = .030, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .01. Because Drew et al. (2017) do not report this measure, no effect size from the original is available for comparison. The cumulative measure we preregistered grew with MSS as well, replicating both versions reported in the original. Averaged over every distractor, it grew from approximately 53 ms at MSS = 1 to approximately 327 ms at MSS = 100 on target absent trials (53 and 291 ms in the original), F(1.71, 63.14) = 157.21, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .56, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .37; averaged over fixated distractors only, it also grew with MSS, F(1.92, 71.18) = 79.73, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .37, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .24. However, that growth on its own favors neither account. The systematic growth in the duration of individual fixations is what a memory search conducted at each fixated item predicts. Figure 4 illustrates how accumulated dwell time on a representative target absent display grows with MSS.

Distractor revisits.

The complementary prediction of the singlevisualsearch account is that participants should rarely return to a distractor: once a distractor has been compared against the entire memory set, there is little reason to look at it again. If, however, participants are conducting multiple visual scans, they should revisit a substantial proportion of distractors, with that proportion growing as MSS grows.

For each trial, we computed the proportion of fixated distractors that received more than one fixation, averaged these proportions across trials within each MSS × target presence cell for each participant, and analyzed the resulting means with a 4 × 2 repeatedmeasures ANOVA. As shown in Figure 3 C, revisit rates were higher on target absent than target present trials, F(1, 37) = 105.65, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .33, and grew with MSS, F(2.61, 96.47) = 105.31, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .46, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .19, with a significant MSS × target presence interaction, F(2.65, 97.99) = 45.68, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .14. Drew et al. (2017) also report the closely related rate of visits per distractor, computed over every distractor in the display rather than only those that were fixated. That measure grew with MSS as well, F(2.47, 91.21) = 249.24, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .65, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .36, from 0.28 visits per distractor at MSS = 1 to 1.28 at MSS = 100 on target absent trials, against the 0.26 and 1.27 reported originally. Even at the largest MSS, however, the proportion of fixated distractors that were revisited remained modest (see Figure 3 C), and items were still not fixated twice on average, well below the level predicted if participants performed a separate visual search for each memorized target. Together with the dwelltime results, this pattern supports the "one visual search, many memory searches" account. MSS = 16 MSS = 100 MSS = 1 MSS = 4 Dwell Time (ms) 0 100 200 300 400 500 Figure 4. Heatmaps of average cumulative dwell time across all participants on a representative targetabsent search display at each memory set size. Warmer colors indicate longer accumulated dwell time within a 100pixel (≈2.6° of visual angle) radius of the cell. The area with dwell time at the middle of the left edge is the location of the button to indicate no target present. Proportion of distractors fixated & functional visual field. One consequence of holding multiple search targets in memory during visual search is that processing demands increase and the ability to inspect items that are peripheral to fixation could decrease. Following Drew et al. (2017), we tested this in two ways: first we examined the proportion of distractors fixated on each trial. Then we measured the functional visual field (FVF), which is an estimate of the area around each fixation in which targets can be detected without refixating.

For each trial, we computed the proportion of distractors that received at least one fixation, averaged these proportions across trials within each MSS × target presence cell for each participant, and analyzed the resulting means with a 4 × 2 repeatedmeasures ANOVA. As shown in Figure 3 A, participants fixated a higher proportion of distractors on target absent than target present trials, F(1, 37) = 1430.85, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .70, and on trials with larger MSS, F(2.67, 98.93) = 410.21, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .80, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .55. The MSS × target presence interaction was also significant, F(2.22, 81.98) = 48.86, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .15. Although the proportion fixated did grow with MSS, the magnitude of the increase was far smaller than the increase predicted by an account in which participants conduct a separate visual search for each memorized target (see Figure 3 A).

To examine how functional processing of the visual scene changed as the number of memo rized items grew, we estimated the FVF on each target present trial as the smallest radius around the trial's fixation points that encompassed 50% of the distractors in the display.

We computed each participant's mean FVF radius (in degrees of visual angle) at each MSS and tested for an effect of MSS with a oneway repeatedmeasures ANOVA. As shown in Figure 5 A, the FVF shrank as MSS grew, F(2.71, 100.26) = 224.71, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .79, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .81, replicating the pattern reported by Drew et al. (2017). This narrowing aligns with the dwelltime and search slope results: as memory load increases, evaluating a peripheral item becomes more costly and participants rely more on direct fixation to evaluate items.

The radiusbased FVF estimate is meaningful only on target present trials, where the stopping rule that motivates the 50% criterion applies. On target absent trials, we instead asked the comple mentary question: given each participant's MSSspecific FVF radius (estimated from target present trials), what proportion of distractors on a target absent trial fell within that radius of any fixation? This proportion is called coverage. If FVF estimates generalize to target absent trials, coverage should be high, since participants should keep searching the display until they are confident no target is present. We tested for an effect of MSS on each participant's mean coverage with a one way repeatedmeasures ANOVA. As shown in Figure 5 B, coverage was somewhat lower for the MSS=1 condition, F(1.93, 71.23) = 33.26, p < .001, 𝜂 2 𝐺 = .36, 𝜂 2 𝐺 𝑂 = .08, but remained high across all conditions, suggesting that the FVF estimates from target present trials generalize reasonably well to target absent trials and that participants did effectively scan the full display when no target was present.

Discussion

Our results replicate Drew et al. (2017). Dwell time per fixation on fixated objects grew with the number of memorized items, from approximately 178 ms at MSS = 1 to approximately 252 ms at MSS = 100 on target absent trials, while participants revisited only a modest fraction of the distractors they fixated and did not, on average, look at an item twice even when holding 100 objects in memory. Combined with the logarithmic scaling of search time with memory load (Figure 2 A), these results reproduce the patterns that Drew et al. (2017) took to support a single visual search combined with repeated memory searches. While all of the theoretically relevant results replicated, there were a few differences in perfor mance between our sample and Drew et al. (2017). Participants in our study were more accurate: at MSS = 100 they responded correctly on 92% of trials, compared with 86% in the original. Their search slopes were also steeper. At MSS = 100, slopes were 405 ms/item on target absent trials and 203 ms/item on target present trials, compared with approximately 285 and 150 ms/item in the original (estimated from their Figure 3). In terms of eye movements, our participants refixated fewer distractors: at MSS = 100, 37% of fixated distractors on target absent trials were fixated again, compared with 60% in the original, and looked at more items in target absent trials (coverage was in the 8696% range in our sample, but around 75% in Drew et al. (2017)). This pattern of differences is consistent with our sample leaning more towards accuracy in the classic speedaccuracy tradeoff, which includes spending more search time on more items.

Our results strengthen the overall evidence for the "one visual search, many memory searches" account in two ways. First, a successful replication with more than double the participants in a different population with different equipment is evidence that the effects are reliable and robust to some sources of variation. Second, our analysis of dwell time is more appropriate for differentiating competing ideas about how hybrid search works. Dwell time per fixation should increase as the number of memorized items increases if each fixation involves searching through memorized items. On the other hand, cumulative dwell time could increase with a larger set of memorized items simply because items are revisited more often. We do, however, note that "one visual search" is maybe a bit of a misnomer here, since refixations were not uncommon. Drew et al. (2017) suggest that this could be because of partial forgetting during the search. Participants may be executing a single visual search, but occasionally forget where they have already looked.

Finally, returning to the pedagogical motivations for running this study, we want to highlight some of the benefits of conducting replication work in an undergraduate research methods course. Like others who have adopted similar approaches (Boyce et al., 2023; de Leeuw, Andrews, Livingston, et al., 2019; Frank & Saxe, 2012; Hawkins et al., 2018; Wagge et al., 2019), we have found the learning opportunities are myriad: students had to closely read empirical work to be able to reimplement every detail; they learned how to organize and execute a team research project, how to use an eye tracker, and how to analyze data in R to generate a reproducible manuscript. We use the process of conducting a publishable replication as a way to focus on concepts related to executing an experiment, while setting aside questions about designing a good experiment to answer a wellmotivated research question. We turn to those challenging topics in the second half of the course, once students have some research experience. Finally, despite the overwhelm ingly successful replication, the results provided moments for us to think critically about what a successful replication entails and how to use theory to understand what kinds of variation in data are relevant to scientific questions.

We gave the LLM access to our experiment code, pre registration, analysis files, and draft of the manuscript and asked it to identify any discrepancies and suggest edits for clarity. Figure 1.

Figure 1. Behavioral task. In each of the four blocks, participants completed a memorization, recognition, and search task. During memorization, participants viewed each object for 3 seconds.During recognition, participants saw every memorized target plus an equal number of foils one at a time, responding by keypress; they had to pass two such tests at 90% accuracy or better before moving on. During search, they clicked the memorized object when one was present or the "Absent" button at the left of the screen when none was. The search task shows scenes from the experiment at actual scale relative to the screen; the text and images of the other panels are not to scale in order to facilitate readability.

Figure 2.

Figure 2. Search time and accuracy. Panel (A) shows that search times are longer with larger memorized set sizes and that this increase is better fit by a logarithmic function than a linear one. Panel (B) shows that the error rate increases with larger memorized set sizes, but is generally quite low. Panel (C) shows a strong relationship between the number of memorized items and the search slope; as the memorized set size increases, search times increase more with each additional item in the visual display.

Figure 3 .Figure 4.

Figure 3. Fixation patterns in the eye tracking data. Panel (A) shows that more distractors are fixated in target absent trials and in trials with larger memorized sets. Panel (B) shows that dwell time per fixation on fixated items increases with larger memorized sets. Panel (C) shows that participants revisit fixated distractors more often with larger memorized set sizes and on target absent trials, but that less than half of the fixated items are revisited.

Figure 5.

Figure 5. (A) Functional visual field estimates on target present trials and (B) estimates of object coverage in target absent trials.
